Anonymous Abstract Artificial general intelligence (AGI) requires method that can understand, generate, reason, and act across diverse domains and modalities. Rather than relying on single model to master every capability, promising path toward AGI now is to orchestrate heterogeneous ecosystem of specialized models, tools, and policies (candidates) into unified AI system. At the heart of this vision lies fundamental question: how can an AI system dynamically select and coordinate the most capable candidate for each task while balancing performance and computational cost? In this work, we present Route Anything, unified framework for studying model and policy routing across generalpurpose AI systems. We develop unified theoretical foundation that characterizes optimal routing under qualitycost trade-offs and establishes how the information available to router determines its attainable performance. To systematically investigate routing across diverse capabilities, we first construct an omni-modal trajectory matrix spanning 25 benchmarks across language understanding, image, video, and speech generation, coding and workplace agents, and robotic manipulation, comprising 374,401 scored runs from 452 candidate configurations. Building on this framework, we train and test different routers to investigate the potential of omni-modal model routing, the capabilities of learned routers, and the conditions under which routing improves efficiency. As result, tool interfaces may not match real ones, tool compositions are not grounded in execution, and tasks rarely span more than one environment. We propose ToolSpace, tool-centric framework that reverses this order. ToolSpace mines data, state, and retry dependencies from adjacent calls in execution traces of real MCP services and open agent corpora, using deterministic rules rather than LLM judgment, and clusters the resulting weighted tool-composition graph into environments. Databases are synthesized only for state-mutating environments, while query and stateless tools replay recorded returns. ToolSpace then performs state-aware random walks with rollback and decompiles each chain into natural-language task with assertion-based checks, keeping only samples that replay successfully from the initial state. Text:"WebHarbor: Docking Real Websites for Evolving GUI Agent Environments WebHarbor Team and Contributors (cid:128) Project Page Data & Models CodeGUI web agents require realistic and interactive environments that remain stable during both evaluation and training. Live website evaluation suffers from changing content, access restrictions, and limited coverage of authenticated workflows, while existing synthetic environments for training often lack functional depth and visual fidelity. We propose WebHarbor, suite of 153 executable and dockerized website environments that faithfully reconstruct the visual interfaces, content, and functional workflows of real-world web services under deterministic control. We build WebHarbor through task-driven human-agent collaboration, where environments evolve as new tasks expand their functional scope, coding agents implement the required changes, and human contributors audit visual fidelity, functional correctness, and task validity. The resulting suite comprises 3,518 tasks spanning multi-step and post-authentication workflows, where existing works often fail to cover. Beyond reliable evaluation, WebHarbor provides high-fidelity synthetic web environments for cost-efficient reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate that Qwen 4B-level GUI agent trained with RL in WebHarbor generalizes to unseen tasks and performs competitively with leading open-source GUI web agents across four benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/aiming-lab/WebHarbor.Web agents powered by large language models (LLMs) with multimodal capability interpret rendered webpages and complete tasks through browser interactions (Deng et al., 2023; He et al., 2024; Zheng et al., 2024; Qin et al., 2025). Their progress depends on environments that support reliable evaluation and training. Evaluation requires stable and reproducible outcomes, while reinforcement learning (RL) additionally requires executable state transitions and repeatable resets (Qi et al., 2025; Bai et al., 2024; Wei et al., 2025; Lai et al., 2025). Live websites offer realistic interfaces, but changing content, bot detection, and regional restrictions undermine experimental control. Moreover, live-web benchmarks rarely cover authenticated workflows, despite their importance to many everyday user requests (He et al., 2024; Xue et al., 2025). In our experiments, about 38% of Online-Mind2Web tasks were blocked by bot detection, and 22% of WebVoyager tasks were outdated due to website changes. These failures introduce uncontrolled variation into evaluation and prevent agents from learning the many workflows hidden behind authentication. An alternative should combine realistic visual observations, functional interactions, and persistent authenticated state with offline execution and reliable resets. Achieving this balance is challenging: commercial websites encompass extensive content and functionality, making complete reconstruction impractical, while visually similar replicas may lack correct search, account management, booking, or transaction logic. Even apparently functional environments may expose shortcuts, omit plausible alternative actions, or leak answers through their interfaces, undermining task validity. The goal is to match both what agents observe and how websites respond to their actions, while ensuring reproducibility and practical utility for both evaluation and RL. Human crafted benchmarks such as WebArena and VisualWebArena offer reproducible applications but cover very few websites (Zhou et al., 2024; Koh et al., 2024). Manually reconstructing real websites can improve fidelity, but requires substantial effort and remains difficult to scale. Recent See full author list in Appendix A. 1 Preprint. Text:"Shall We Approve? Consequence-Aware Approval for Autonomous Coding Agents Weiheng Wu, Junkai Chen, Zhensu Sun, Jieke Shi, Yunbo Lyu, David LoCoding agents increasingly rely on automated approvers to decide whether proposed shell command may run. Existing approvers, from rule-based checkers to guardrail models and deployed permission classifiers, base their decisions on the text of the request and command. Yet commands behavior depends on the code it invokes and the state of the workspace, and systems that observe file changes leave the assessment to the agent or human after execution. We present Caper, consequenceaware approval mechanism that pre-executes each command in snapshotted sandbox, provides an independent approver with bounded report of its observed effects, commits allowed commands, and rolls back blocked ones with reason the agent can act on. Compared with six existing systems, Caper achieves the lowest harmful-outcome rate on SABER (6.7% versus at least 14.8%) and YoloFS-Ext, benchmark we build in which harmful behavior is hidden inside invoked tools (7.8% versus at least 35.3%), while blocking 1.0% of the safe twins of the dangerous tasks in YoloFS-Ext. github.com/wudaoz/Caper 1. Introduction Autonomous coding agents edit code, run builds and tests, and issue shell commands in stateful workspaces (Yang et al., 2024, Jimenez et al., 2024, Wang et al., 2025, Lyu et al., 2026, 2025). single wrong command can leak credential, delete data, or weaken security control (Qu et al., 2026, Tang et al., 2026). To avoid relying solely on the agents own judgment to prevent such harm, agent frameworks require approval before commands can run. Asking human to approve every command does not scale. Frequent prompts slow development and lead to approval fatigue (Anthropic, 2025), and Anthropic reports that users approve 93% of Claude Code permission prompts (Anthropic, 2026). Systems like Claude Code Auto Mode therefore use allow rules and model-based classifiers to make these decisions (Anthropic, 2026), turning approval into an automated decision at each step of the agents loop. Existing approvers assess the text of an action rather than its effects. Static rule checkers such as CARE match the command string against patterns for dangerous syntax, paths, and provenance (Liu et al., 2026, Chen and Lin, 2026); guardrail models such as ToolSafe, gpt-oss-safeguard, and Qwen3Guard assess the request, the command, or the interaction history against safety policy (Mou et al., 2026, OpenAI, 2025, Zhao et al., 2025); and deployed permission classifiers such as Claude Code Auto Mode combine allow rules with model that reads the visible transcript (Anthropic, 2026). None of these systems observes what the Shall We Approve? Consequence-Aware Approval for Autonomous Coding Agents Figure 1: The same command can have different effects in different workspaces. The user asks the agent to remove build artifacts and recompile, and in both workspaces the agent proposes make clean && make. The workspaces differ only in their Makefile, whose clean target deletes src/ and .git/ in workspace but only build/ in workspace B. An approver that sees only the command cannot tell them apart. proposed command does before making decision. This gap is particularly pronounced for shell commands. file edit specifies its changes in the call, but shell command can invoke code whose behavior is not visible in the command text. project script, build target, or package tool can read credential, delete data, or access the network without any of these actions appearing in the command. An approver that never observes the commands effects in the current workspace can therefore let such harm pass (Zhong et al., 2026). Figure 1 shows how the same command can have different effects. The command make clean && make deletes only regenerable build artifacts in one workspace but deletes source files and repository history in another, because its effects depend on the contents of the Makefile. An approver that judges only the command text sees identical input in both cases. To decide correctly, it needs to observe the commands effects in the current workspace and assess them against the task, since deleting the same files can be requested in one task and harmful in another. Each approval decision must therefore answer one question: what security-relevant effects will this command have on the current workspace, and are they within the scope of the users request? To answer this question, we propose Caper (Consequence-Aware PERmission), which runs each command first and bases its approval decision on the observed effects. Each proposed shell command executes once inside the snapshotted task container, under system-call tracing, before any result reaches the agent. From this pre-execution, the system derives bounded effects report. raw trace is unsuitable because single Shall We Approve? Consequence-Aware Approval for Autonomous Coding Agents command can produce tens of kilobytes of system calls, most of which are irrelevant and some of which are shaped by untrusted content (Debenedetti et al., 2024). The report retains only consequential trace events, namely persistent workspace changes, connection attempts, and command output, each attributed to the process that caused it and the files that process drew on. It also records reads of credentials and other protected data. An LLM approver assesses these consequences against the users task. If it allows the command, the resulting state is kept and the output is returned to the agent; if it blocks the command, the container is restored to the snapshot and the reason is returned, so the agent can seek safer path. Results. We evaluate Caper on SABER (Hu et al., 2026), whose shell-only tasks pair development requests with task-specific harms, and on YoloFS-Ext, our controlled extension of YoloFS (Zhong et al., 2026), in which harmful behavior is hidden in the tool invoked by seemingly benign command. Each dangerous task in YoloFS-Ext has safe twin with the same request and command but harmless tool. Compared with six existing systems, Caper achieves the lowest harmful-outcome rate on SABER (6.7% versus 14.821.2%), while Caper and its Claude Code hook achieve the two lowest rates on YoloFS-Ext (7.8% and 6.9% versus 35.394.1%). The safe-outcome rate on SABER remains at the level achieved without approval. On YoloFSExt, Caper safely completes the largest number of dangerous tasks while blocking 1.0"

Text:"LOLPlay: Benchmarking Real-Time Visual Agents in League of Legends Zhaojian Yu zhaojian.yu01@gmail.comVisual agents need to coordinate reasoning and action while their environment evolves during inference. We introduce LOLPlay, benchmark in native League of Legends with 36 champion-specific tasks spanning resource management, combat, cooperation, and objectives. Agents interact through screenshots and mouse keyboard actions; automated initialization and 82 executable subgoal checks distinguish partial progress from completion. Across 324 task evaluations of three models at three reasoning-effort settings, GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest task success rate of 25.0% at low and medium effort, and the highest subgoal success rate of 40.3% at medium effort. However, high effort improves neither metric over low effort for any model. For Astra, longer requests leave fewer interaction rounds, suggesting cost to additional reasoning in real-time control. Trajectories reveal incomplete subgoal composition and actions executing after the observed scene changes, motivating agents that combine visual reasoning with timely action.1 Figure 1: The agent thinks; the environment keeps moving. Top: schematic environment waiting for an action (e.g., VideoGameBench Lite [33]), not paused-game experiment. Bottom: LOLPlay advances approximately 10 game seconds across reasoning, actions, and capture. 1Project repository: https://github.com/answers111/LOLPlay. Preprint. Figure 2: The LOLPlay execution and evaluation workflow. Task configuration connects screenshot-based interaction to independent state/event checks while gameplay continues. The abridged configuration and unmodified game frame illustrate task 035 (Warwick). Live Client API evidence supplies final/deadline states and time-stamped events for evaluation.An intelligent agent should be able to pursue goal even when the world does not wait for its next decision. For visual agents, which use visual observations to guide their actions, this means reasoning about scene that may change before they can act. Consider an agent preparing an action from screenshot: by the time it finishes reasoning, the target may have moved, an opportunity may have disappeared, or previously safe situation may have become dangerous. The challenge is to keep sequence of decisions aligned with an environment that changes throughout execution. This raises central question: can todays visual agents turn their understanding of scene into timely actions that accomplish sustained goal? To study this question, we introduce LOLPlay, benchmark for real-time visual agents in live League of Legends. It evaluates whether agents can combine timely interaction with the completion of multiple task requirements while gameplay continues throughout reasoning and execution. Explicit task outcomes and subgoal checks make both complete success and the gap between local progress and unfinished goals measurable. Progress in computer-use and game evaluation provides the foundation for this work. OSWorld [30] and OSWorld 2.0 [32] assess tasks in real applications and extended workflows; VideoGameBench [33] and GameWorld [19] examine interaction with evolving game environments. LOLPlay focuses on champion-specific objectives in native multiplayer online battle arena (MOBA), where resource acquisition, combat, survival, and objective sequencing can constrain the same task. Table 1 compares task settings and evaluation evidence. The MOBA setting makes these demands concrete. successful action is rarely an isolated achievement: reaching target, choosing an ability, and preserving enough health to continue must work together. Meanwhile, units move and combat proceeds without waiting for the agent. An action planned for one encounter may arrive after that encounter has ended (Figure 1). Even when an agent secures an intermediate objective, it must adapt its subsequent decisions to complete the remaining task. These episodes expose whether the agent can adjust its behavior as the conditions for success change. 2 Table 1: Comparison with computer-use and game benchmarks. LOLPlay combines championspecific tasks in native MOBA with uninterrupted gameplay and stateand event-based evaluation. Real-time interaction and executable scoring individually have precedents; the table compares task settings and evaluation evidence, not model scores. These tasks turn broad gameplay competence into explicit goals with identifiable requirements. Egocentric human videos offer scalable substitute, but overlaying rendered robots on human videos or retargeting human poses into robot actions leaves significant visual or action gaps for high-DoF robot embodiments. To address this limitation, we propose RoboTryOn, which treats human-to-robot translation as virtual try-on: try-on keeps the person and swaps the garment, RoboTryOn keeps the demonstrated motion and swaps the human for the robot. Specifically, RoboTryOn converts human video into video of the target robot specified by reference image, while preserving the original task, objects, and background. To maintain visual consistency, we translate selected keyframes autoregressively and use them to guide video synthesis with motion conditioned on the human hand skeleton. Additionally, we use an inverse dynamics model (IDM) that incorporates depth features to infer action labels from synthesized videos for policy learning. Our experiments across multiple humanoid embodiments show that policies trained with our synthetic data improve the average success rate on novel tasks, including challenging bimanual manipulation, from 2% to 60%, without any real robot demonstration of those tasks. Moreover, training with our synthetic data matches the performance of policies trained solely on teleoperated demonstrations at approximately third of the collection cost, demonstrating the cost efficiency of our approach. 1. Synthesis has mostly added work, not depth: 94% of the solved synthetic tasks pass within three turns, yet depth, unlike the turn count, predicts difficulty beyond work. Text:"What We Keep of What We Forget: Replacement Operators and Routing Semantics in Training-Free MoE Expert Pruning Ezza Ali 1 2 Sehrish Bibi 2 Momina Moetesum 1 2 Faisal Shafait1. Introduction Training-free expert pruning makes two decisions: which experts to remove, and what the layer computes when its router still selects removed expert. Published methods change both at once. Holding the mask fixed, we compare replacement operators from three families on OLMoE, DeepSeek-V2-Lite and Moonlight, with Granite as fourth router, using paired, multiplicitycorrected tests. The largest effect is choice the survivors gate scale. usually left implicit: On routers trained without top-k renormalization, renormalizing the survivors gates, common implementation choice, costs 0.88.5 points and reverses whether rerouting beats MoNEs meanoutput replacement; on two routers trained with renormalization, the trained scale wins at 50% pruning. We introduce the tilted novice, closedform low-rank map of the pruned experts input whose rank-0 case is MoNEs constant. At 50% pruning it adds 1.21.8% parameters and gains 3.45 and 1.37 points over the constant on OLMoE and Moonlight, stable across calibration draws and largely unchanged by 4and 3-bit quantization. No operator dominates: trained-scale rerouting is best on DeepSeek-V2-Lite, and partial copy of each pruned expert is statistically indistinguishable from the tilted novice on two models. Evaluation can change conclusions: at 25% pruning MoNEs constant keeps 9297% of dense multiple-choice accuracy but only 565% of GSM8K and 017% of code scores, and the calibration domain moves the mask 49 times more than resampling. We propose five-item reporting checklist. 1School of Electrical Engineering and Computer Science (SEECS), National University of Sciences and Technology (NUST) 2Deep Learning Laboratory, National Center of Artificial Intelligence (NCAI), NUST. Correspondence to: Ezza Ali <eali.phdai25seecs@seecs.edu.pk>. Preprint. November 2, 2026. 1 Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) layers let language model hold many more parameters than it uses for any one token (Shazeer et al., 2017; Lepikhin et al., 2021; Fedus et al., 2022). Recent open models use fine-grained designs: dozens to hundreds of small routed experts per layer, top-k router, and often few always-on shared experts (Dai et al., 2024; DeepSeek-AI, 2024; Muennighoff et al., 2025; Liu et al., 2025). Conventional deployments retain the routed expert weights in accelerator-accessible memory even though each token activates only subset, making expert storage substantial deployment cost. Training-free expert pruning removes selected experts without gradient-based retraining and can be combined with quantization (Lasby et al., 2026; Tseng et al., 2026). pruning method makes two decisions. The first is selection: which experts to remove. It has received almost all of the attention, from routing frequency (Muzio et al., 2024) to router-weighted activation norms (Lasby et al., 2026), unified scoring families (Liu et al., 2026b), second-order interaction objectives (Tseng et al., 2026), reconstructionbased search (Lu et al., 2024; Li et al., 2026a) and learned importance (Bai et al., 2025; Sun et al., 2026). The second is replacement: what the layer computes when the router still selects removed expert. Methods answer it differently, mostly implicitly. REAP and HOPE drop the expert and renormalize the surviving gates. MoNE keeps the routing slot and substitutes the experts mean output (Zhang et al., 2026). MoRA substitutes retained expert passed through fitted affine map (Sun et al., 2026), ConMoE hands the slot to retained expert with the original gate (Yao et al., 2026), and COMPEL adds share of the pruned experts weights into the survivors (Yoon & Choi, 2026). Because methods differ in both decisions at once, published comparison of pruning methods is also an uncontrolled comparison of replacement operators. The choice is also entering deployment formats: in October 2026, vLLMs compression tooling added checkpoint schema for REAP-style pruned and MoNE-style novice experts.1 The distinction itself is 1https://github.com/vllm-project/ compressed-tensors/pull/938 //github.com/vllm-project/llm-compressor/ and https: Replacement Operators and Routing Semantics in Training-Free MoE Expert Pruning Table 1. Text:"SeMo3D: Unifying 3D Generation, Editing, and Completion via Selective Modeling Xiaokun Sun, Jian Yang, Zhenyu ZhangCorresponding Author xiaokun sun@smail.nju.edu.cn, csjyang@nju.edu.cn, zhangjesse@foxmail.com Project Page: https://xiaokunsun.github.io/SeMo3D.github.io/ Figure 1. SeMo3D unifies 3D generation, editing, and completion via Selective Modeling with image guidance and optional text and points, learning from standard 3D assets without paired editing or completion data. It further supports agentic interactive 3D creation.3D generation, editing, and completion are typically treated as regenerating the complete 3D asset under task-specific constraints, even when only local region needs modification. This leads to task isolation, inconsistency in content that should remain unchanged, and dependence on taskspecific paired data. We address these issues with key observation: the three tasks all synthesize designated region while keeping the rest intactthe full region for generation, the target region for editing, and the missing region for completion. We term this paradigm Selective Modeling and build SeMo3D upon it; it naturally unifies the three tasks, preserves unchanged content by construction, and enables training without paired editing or completion data, since any 3D asset can be partitioned into selected region and its context. For training, we propose region-selective denoising, which randomly selects regions of varying sizes on standard 3D assets and applies noise and loss only to them, jointly learning all three tasks. For architecture, we propose Omni3DiT, multi-condition 3D diffusion transformer that distinguishes regions to create from those to retain and fuses image, text, and point guidance for fine-grained control. Experiments across three benchmarks show SeMo3D surpasses state-of-the-art methods on all tasks, delivering multimodal controllability in generation and strong consistency and accuracy in editing and completion. SeMo3D further serves as unified modeling tool for agents, enabling efficient 3D creation through natural-language interaction. Project page: https://xiaokunsun.github.io/ SeMo3D.github.io/. 1 Figure 2. (a) Existing holistic modeling regenerates the complete asset even for local modification, requiring per-task models and paired editing/completion data, while regions that should remain unchanged are often altered (black boxes). (b) Our selective modeling synthesizes only the designated region, unifying the three tasks, preserving the rest by construction, and needing no task-specific paired data. 1. Introduction 3D content creationspanning generation [36, 40], editing [21], completion [84], and their combinationsis central to games [92], AR/VR [1, 44], robotics [12, 55], and digital twins [47]. Although typically studied in isolation, these tasks are conventionally viewed through the same lens: regenerating complete 3D asset under task-specific constraintsmatching the condition for generation, and additionally the source asset for editing or the partial input for completion. unified framework covering all three within one model is thus appealing yet remains an open problem. Driven by generative models [24, 41] and large-scale 3D datasets [13, 14], all three tasks have advanced rapidly. As illustrated in Fig. 2(a), mainstream feed-forward approaches [11, 56, 72, 7678] share common paradigm we term holistic modeling: creating the complete 3D asset under image or text guidance. To preserve consistency with the source, editing and completion methods [11, 56, 72, 76] additionally condition on the source asset or partial input, yet they still regenerate the entire object even when only local region needs to be modified.1 This creates fundamental mismatch between what needs to change and what is actually modeled, which leads to three key limitations. (1) Task isolation. As each task imposes different constraint, generation [77, 78, 94], editing [46, 72, 76], and 1In this paper, editing refers to local operations like addition, deletion, or replacement, not global operations like style transfer. completion [11, 25, 45] rely on separate models with distinct architectures and objectives, duplicating design and training effort across tasks and multiplying deployment costs. (2) Region inconsistency. Because the full asset is regenerated, source fidelity can only be encouraged rather than guaranteed, and regions that should remain intact are (3) Editfrequently altered (black boxes in Fig. 2(a)). ing/completion data dependency. Regenerating whole asset that respects the source demands sourcetarget supervision, so many editing [19, 72, 76, 93] and completion [11, 25, 45] methods require costly large-scale paired datasets (e.g., PartFlow [72] curates 100K editing pairs via seven-stage pipeline), limiting scalability. Moreover, most methods accept only single guidance modality (image or text), offering limited control: text lacks geometric precision, while images leave occluded regions ambiguous. Our key observation is that, despite their different constraints, the three tasks share common structure: synthesizing designated region while keeping the rest intact the full, target, and missing regions for generation, editing, and completion. This motivates selective modeling: given an asset represented by spatially aligned tokens (e.g., structured latents [77, 78]), the model synthesizes only the selected tokens and reuses the rest (Fig. 2(b)), unlike holistic modeling. Selective modeling thus (1) unifies the three tasks, which differ only in the selected region; (2) preserves unchanged content by construction, as context tokens are 2 never regenerated;2 and (3) requires no task-specific paired data, since any ordinary asset can be partitioned into selected region and its context. This resolves the three limitations above in single paradigm. Based on this insight, we present SeMo3D, multicondition model unifying 3D generation, editing, and completion via Selective Modeling, as illustrated in Fig. 1. We instantiate selective modeling through two key components. On the training side, we propose region-selective denoising: randomly selecting regions of varying sizes on ordinary 3D assets, adding noise only to the selected tokens, and denoising them while keeping the rest clean. As the selected region ranges from small portion to the full region, this single procedure spans the entire task spectrum, from local editing through partial completion to full generation, "

Text:"2026-11-4 You dont need long context Peter Belcak, Saurav Muralidharan, Jindong Jiang, Yonggan Fu, Wonmin Byeon, Hanrong Ye, Ryan Angilly, Ali Taghibakhshi, Shih-Yang Liu, Siru Ouyang, Greg Heinrich, Pavlo Molchanov Abstract. Later tokens are costlier to generate than earlier tokens. On frontier open weight models, the disparity in cost may rise to as much as 7x. Through frequent compaction, we show across broad range of long-horizon and long-input tasks that frontier language models sacrifice little to no task accuracy when maintaining short contexts. We further demonstrate that LLM performance does not collapse on long-horizon software engineering tasks even when taking at most one action per context, and based on that we outline the concept of semantic state space models (SSSMs). Our findings question the need for long-context language models. Links: Lab Project Moonshot: Local Frontier Intelligence 1. Introduction Not all tokens are generated equal. Token generation latency and memory footprint both grow with the number of tokens in language models context. This is due to the inherent linear1 relationship between the computational cost of generating token and its depth within context [25]. Figure 1 shows the cloud token generation cost as function of context window size for selection of popular open-weight models. Observe that there is considerable monetary benefit to serving models on cloud GPUs with consistently shorter contexts. This fact is often hidden from the end user since retail token prices are quoted at fixed price per 1M tokens regardless of the input context length. Note that the extent of cost reductions (3.6 to 7) rivals or exceeds that of many other orthogonal optimization methods such as aggressive quantization or structured sparsification [5, 14, 8]. Monetary cost is not the only quality of LM workloads that suffers when inputs are long. Figure 1 The cost of long context. Dollar cost of generating 1M output tokens on an existing input KV cache "

Text:"2026-11-4 You dont need long context Peter Belcak, Saurav Muralidharan, Jindong Jiang, Yonggan Fu, Wonmin Byeon, Hanrong Ye, Ryan Angilly, Ali Taghibakhshi, Shih-Yang Liu, Siru Ouyang, Greg Heinrich, Pavlo Molchanov Abstract. Later tokens are costlier to generate than earlier tokens. On frontier open weight models, the disparity in cost may rise to as much as 7x. Through frequent compaction, we show across broad range of long-horizon and long-input tasks that frontier language models sacrifice little to no task accuracy when maintaining short contexts. We further demonstrate that LLM performance does not collapse on long-horizon software engineering tasks even when taking at most one action per context, and based on that we outline the concept of semantic state space models (SSSMs). Our findings question the need for long-context language models. Links: Lab Project Moonshot: Local Frontier Intelligence 1. Introduction Not all tokens are generated equal. Token generation latency and memory footprint both grow with the number of tokens in language models context. This is due to the inherent linear1 relationship between the computational cost of generating token and its depth within context [25]. Figure 1 shows the cloud token generation cost as function of context window size for selection of popular open-weight models. Observe that there is considerable monetary benefit to serving models on cloud GPUs with consistently shorter contexts. This fact is often hidden from the end user since retail token prices are quoted at fixed price per 1M tokens regardless of the input context length. Note that the extent of cost reductions (3.6 to 7) rivals or exceeds that of many other orthogonal optimization methods such as aggressive quantization or structured sparsification [5, 14, 8]. Monetary cost is not the only quality of LM workloads that suffers when inputs are long. Figure 1 The cost of long context. Dollar cost of generating 1M output tokens on an existing input KV cache as function of the context window size, obtained by converting the measured peak decode throughput of each deployment at on-demand H100 prices ($3.85 per GPU-hour, Nebius, Sept. 2026). Input request lengths are drawn uniformly at random. GLM 5.2 and Kimi K2.6 are served with INT4 weights and BF16 activations, Qwen 3 Coder Next is served in BF16. Serving at context window size of 256K rather than 16K costs 3.6 more per generated token for GLM, 5.6 more for Kimi, and 7 more for Qwen. 1Model architectures with constant cost of token generation with respect to context depth have been repeatedly proposed but have never been fully embraced linear-cost attention-based mechanisms remain the backbone of all popular language models. 2026 NVIDIA. All rights reserved. You dont need long context Figure 2 The burden of long context. The token generation latency, token throughput, and memory footprint of several open-source models measured on an NVIDIA DGX Spark plotted as function of input context length. For several models, later tokens take approximately 2x longer to generate than earlier tokens and require larger memory footprint. Figure 2 shows the token generation latency, throughput, and memory footprint of several popular locally-run coding models as function of the input context length. Observe how the generation inter-token latency approximately doubles for several models across the measured context lengths, implying doubling of the time required to generate response at longer contexts. Put simply, the longer the input context provided to the language model (LM), the slower, more memory hungry, and more expensive its output token generation. Given the considerable benefits of keeping input context short in both cloud and edge LM use, one would assume that popular LMs would have naturally evolved to operate on contexts only as short as necessary to complete their tasks. This could not be farther from the truth. As seen in Table 1, which compares the lengths of well-known works of writing with the context window sizes of representative proprietary and open-weight models, context window sizes have vastly outgrown many influential writings. It would seem that models have been evolving to accommodate the ever larger possible inputs rather than to maximize efficiency. The enormous context window sizes of contemporary LMs and the tangible cost advantages of keeping their contexts short thus appear to be at odds with each other. While operating with longer contexts might in some cases be thought beneficial to model task accuracy, we must ask: RQ0 Are long contexts necessary? If not, lets do away with long contexts and long-context models. And, if there are currently any objective benefits of keeping long context models, we can likely preserve them by making the appropriate post-training adjustments to future models. Literary works Model context window sizes Work Words Tokens Model Words Tokens 27K 48K 37K MiniMax M2.7 [31] The Old Man and the Sea [21] 64K Claude Opus 4.5 [2] The Great Gatsby [15] Quran (Saheeh Intl translation) [38] 156K 207K Gemma 4 31B [16] 181K 241K Kimi K2.6 [32] King James New Testament [26] 184K 245K Qwen 3 Coder Next [9] Great Expectations [12] 481K 640K Gemini 3.8 Flash [13] The Lord of the Rings [42] 566K 753K GLM 5.2 [51] War and Peace [43] 603K 802K GPT 5.6 Sol [34] King James Old Testament [26] 150K 150K 192K 192K 197K 750K 750K 790K 200K 200K 256K 256K 262K 1M 1M 1.05M Table 1 Lengths of literary works against model context window sizes. Word counts use the cited English editions. Word-token conversions use approximately 1.33 tokens per English word, while actual counts vary with text and tokenizer. Models and their context window sizes are detailed in Section 2.3. 2 You dont need long context To answer RQ0, look at where the so-called long contexts (and, correspondingly, large context windows) in language modeling come from. Broadly, there are two types of workloads that often result in long input contexts being passed to LMs: LCW1 Workloads of long-horizon tasks tasks that may require many actions in order to be completed and where the context builds up as the model makes its way towards the completion of the task [11, 47, 52]. LCW2 Workloads of long-input tasks tasks that require operating across large amount of input information [6, 45, 29]. Examining these two types of workloads more closely, it is not immediately clear whether operating on long contexts is necessary in order to complete them. I"

We introduce GigaSpeech 3, corpus of 39,000 hours and 10 million weakly transcribed speech segments covering Algerian, Egyptian, Iraqi, Saudi, and Syrian Arabic. Starting from 60 hours of manual transcription per dialect, we train labeling models using speech-recognition-biased self-supervised learning and apply them to dialect-matched web audio. Data selection and four rounds of noisy-student refinement for Saudi and Syrian Arabic yield the final corpus. We validate its utility through dialect-specific and multi-dialect speech recognition training and cross-domain evaluation. On GigaSpeechBench, the final dialect-specific models outperform their labeling models across all five dialects and achieve macro-average word error rate of 23.86%, 16.6% relative reduction from the strongest third-party system covering all five dialects, while outperforming the best third-party result on three dialects. multi-dialect model using self-predicted dialect identities achieves macroaverage word error rate of 25.51% while also supporting Modern Standard Arabic. Abstract Route Anything is a framework for dynamically selecting and coordinating specialized AI models and policies across diverse tasks to improve performance and efficiency.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Artificial general intelligence (AGI) requires a method that can understand, generate, reason, and act across diverse domains and modalities. Rather than relying on a single model to master every capability, a promising path toward AGI now is to orchestrate a heterogeneous ecosystem of specialized models, tools, and policies (candidates) into a unified AI system. At the heart of this vision lies a fundamental question: how can an AI system dynamically select and coordinate the most capable candidate for each task while balancing performance and computational cost? In this work, we present Route Anything, a unified framework for studying model and policy routing across general-purpose AI systems. We develop a unified theoretical foundation that characterizes optimal routing under quality--cost trade-offs and establishes how the information available to a router determines its attainable performance. To systematically investigate routing across diverse capabilities, we first construct an omni-modal trajectory matrix spanning 25 benchmarks across language understanding, image, video, and speech generation, coding and workplace agents, and robotic manipulation, comprising 374,401 scored runs from 452 candidate configurations. Building on this framework, we train and test different routers to investigate the potential of omni-modal model routing, the capabilities of learned routers, and the conditions under which routing improves efficiency. Our results reveal substantial opportunities for capability orchestration: per-input oracle routing outperforms the best fixed candidate by 5.4 points, and learned routers outperform it by 0.3 points across 16 benchmarks, by 2.1 points on video generation, and by 1.1 points on robot manipulation while using 24% less policy time. More importantly, routing raises efficiency by 8.82x on average compared with the reference model.

Авторы строят теоретическую основу маршрутизации с учётом компромисса между качеством и вычислительной стоимостью и показывают, как информация, доступная роутеру, ограничивает достижимую производительность. Для экспериментов собрана omni-modal trajectory matrix из 25 бенчмарков (текст, изображения, видео, речь, код, робототехника) и 374 401 оценённых запусков от 452 конфигураций. Результаты показывают, что обученные роутеры превосходят лучшую фиксированную модель, а маршрутизация повышает эффективность в среднем в 8.82 раза по сравнению с референсной моделью.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Не одна модель, а оркестр специалистов: умная маршрутизация повышает качество и экономит ресурсы"}

Abstract Pandora is a psychotherapy client simulation framework that models evolving psychological and relational states to generate more authentic, individually aligned expressions and reduce AI-like responses.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Psychotherapy clients may experience distress before they can coherently describe it, and their expression changes with emotion, perceived safety, and trust. Large language model-based client simulators often rely on clinically organized case descriptions, which can encourage responses explaining the client's difficulties more clearly than their current understanding warrants. We propose Pandora, a psychotherapy client simulation framework that models how psychological experience becomes situated, individual expression. Pandora updates psychological and relational states in response to therapist interventions, constructs subjective appraisals and attributions, and plans response content and strategy. A separate expression planner combines these decisions with language patterns distilled from the client's utterances to guide linguistic realization. We evaluate Pandora against five baselines on 115 cases from four counseling and motivational interviewing datasets. It achieves the highest expression similarity and overall disclosure-alignment score among the compared systems. In a four-evaluator study of 45 matched disclosure items, Pandora receives AI-like judgments in 7.2% of ratings, compared with 43.3--67.2% for the baselines and 35.6% without expression planning. The ablation also reduces expression similarity. These results support explicit expression planning in client simulation to improve alignment with individual language patterns and reduce perceived AI-like expression in the evaluated setting.

Система обновляет эти состояния после вмешательств терапевта, формирует субъективные оценки и атрибуции, а отдельный expression planner переводит решения в язык, опираясь на языковые паттерны конкретного клиента. На 115 кейсах из четырёх датасетов Pandora показала наибольшее сходство выражений и лучшее соответствие раскрытия, а в оценке людьми доля AI-like ответов составила всего 7.2% против 43.3–67.2% у baseline-моделей. Abstract LoCo uses local preference learning to teach reasoning models when to stop deliberating, reducing output length substantially while preserving or improving accuracy across math and code tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large reasoning models achieve strong performance on challenging tasks, but their long chain-of-thought often incurs substantial inference costs by continuing to deliberate after sufficient information has been obtained. Existing reasoning compression methods primarily operate on reasoning trajectories, paying less attention to the local decision of whether further reasoning is necessary given the current state. We propose LoCo, a local preference learning method for reasoning compression. LoCo detects candidate overthinking states from the model's own traces, freezes the corresponding reasoning prefixes, regenerates shorter verified continuations from the same states, and trains on the resulting state-conditioned preference pairs with DPO. This formulation teaches the model when to continue reasoning and when to conclude, rather than simply encouraging shorter trajectories overall. We construct training data entirely from self-generated mathematical traces and evaluate LoCo on five mathematical and three code generation benchmarks. LoCo reduces completion length by 41.7\%--45.4\% on mathematics and 23.4\%--36.3\% on code, while maintaining or improving accuracy and achieving the best AUC across all code benchmarks. LoCo consistently improves the accuracy-efficiency trade-off on mathematics and transfers effectively to code generation despite math-only training, demonstrating the cross-domain generality of local preference learning for reasoning compression. The code and model will be publicly released at https://github.com/MuyaoYuan/LoCo.
[04.11.2026 12:49] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет LoCo — метод локального обучения предпочтениям (local preference learning) для сжатия рассуждений больших reasoning-моделей. Метод находит состояния, где модель избыточно «думает», замораживает префиксы рассуждений и генерирует более короткие проверенные продолжения, после чего обучает модель через DPO на парах предпочтений. Так модель учится понимать, когда продолжать рассуждение, а когда завершать его, а не просто сокращать все цепочки рассуждений целиком. Обучение проводится только на математических данных, но метод заметно сокращает длину ответов как в математике, так и в задачах генерации кода, сохраняя или улучшая точность.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Учим модель вовремя остановиться и дать ответ"}

Abstract DreamX leverages a pretrained video diffusion model to learn compact, appearance-invariant latent actions that enable cross-embodiment transfer and efficient policy learning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Learning a universal action representation from video requires modeling dynamics that are consistent across different executions of the same action yet invariant to changes in embodiment and appearance. Existing latent action models infer actions from visual transitions, but their representations often entangle the underlying action with irrelevant visual variations, limiting transfer across embodiments. We introduce DreamX, which repurposes a pretrained video diffusion model as a universal latent action learner. Our approach extracts compact latent actions from the video model's rich spatiotemporal features, compressing each latent video frame by 300times (1{,200 rightarrow 4} tokens). In addition, by leveraging the video model's generative prior, these latent actions capture action-relevant dynamics disentangled from visual appearance. The resulting latent space serves as a shared action interface across embodiments (human and robot), environments, and action parameterizations. On LARY-Bench, DreamX achieves state-of-the-art performance among latent action models. Moreover, actions encoded in one setting remain effective in another: the latent space supports any-to-any video transfer and zero-shot action transfer across diverse embodiments and environments. Furthermore, this shared interface also enables policy learning in a compact latent action space, reducing the computational cost of video-diffusion-based policies.
[04.11.2026 12:49] Response: {"desc": "DreamX использует предобученную video diffusion модель как универсальный обучатель латентных действий, извлекая из её пространственно-временных признаков компактные представления, которые сжимают каждый кадр видео в 300 раз (с 1200 до 4 токенов). Благодаря генеративному prior модели эти латентные действия отделяют динамику, связанную с действием, от визуального внешнего вида, что делает их инвариантными к embodiment и окружению. Полученное латентное пространство служит общим интерфейсом для людей, роботов и разных параметризаций действий, обеспечивая state-of-the-art результаты на LARY-Bench, any-to-any перенос видео и zero-shot перенос действий. Abstract Axion 1.0 is an open agent foundation model trained via a concise three-stage pipeline of supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, achieving competitive agentic performance while fully releasing its data and training recipe.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small We introduce Axion 1.0, a fully open agent foundation model that combines strong and broad agentic capabilities with a remarkably simple training recipe. Axion 1.0 is trained in only three stages: two lightweight supervised fine-tuning stages followed by reinforcement learning, without dedicated mid-training, direct preference optimization (DPO), or on-policy distillation (OPD). The first SFT stage develops strong self-contained reasoning through model-aware data construction, where we synthesize challenging problems and adversarially strengthen them based on the base model's own successful solution strategies. The second stage extends these capabilities to tool-dependent reasoning through a scalable pipeline for constructing diverse and automatically verifiable tasks grounded in real-world data sources and deterministic computation. Finally, reinforcement learning further strengthens long-horizon reasoning and interaction with external tools and environments. Despite this compact training recipe, Axion 1.0 achieves strong performance across a broad suite of agentic benchmarks and is competitive with leading commercial models on several of them. Equally importantly, Axion 1.0 is open beyond model weights: we release the complete recipe, including data construction, generation, filtering, verification, prompts, and training code, enabling the entire development process to be reproduced and extended. Axion 1.0 demonstrates that strong agent foundation models can be both open and simple, offering a practical and reproducible path toward advancing open agentic intelligence.

Abstract FactEmbed improves composed image retrieval by decoupling query understanding from embedding construction through factorized latents that simulate visual modifications before retrieval.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Composed Image Retrieval (CIR) aims to retrieve a target image given a reference image and a modification text. Although recent approaches leveraging Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown significant progress, we argue that the MLLM's compositional understanding remains underutilized. This stems from directly mapping composed queries into a retrieval embedding via contrastive supervision alone, without explicitly resolving the intended visual modifications. In this work, we introduce Factorized MLLM Embeddings (FactEmbed), which decouples compositional query comprehension from building discriminative embeddings. Rather than directly projecting the composed query into a single embedding without internal reasoning, FactEmbed employs a set of factorized latents that simulate the intended visual modifications via an intermediate target imagination objective. This process encourages the latents to ground into target semantics before forming discriminative representations for retrieval. Subsequently, to propagate these factorized representations throughout the model for retrieval, we optimize a factor-level contrastive loss while distilling the decomposed representations into a deeper attention layer of the MLLM. Finally, for efficient inference, we retain the [EOS] token to establish a global-level embedding on top of these factorized latents, while we exploit the mean-pooled factorized latents to rerank a short candidate list at negligible cost, as both are produced by the same forward pass. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that FactEmbed outperforms prior works across CIR benchmarks.
[04.11.2026 12:49] Response: {"desc": "FactEmbed — это новый метод для Composed Image Retrieval (CIR), где по опорному изображению и текстовому описанию изменений ищется целевая картинка. Авторы утверждают, что существующие подходы на базе MLLM не используют композиционное понимание модели в полной мере, так как запрос напрямую отображается в эмбеддинг только через контрастивную supervision. Предложенный метод разделяет понимание запроса и построение дискриминативных эмбеддингов: набор факторизованных латентов имитирует целевые визуальные модификации через промежуточную цель target imagination, а затем factor-level contrastive loss и дистилляция в более глубокий attention-слой MLLM распространяют эти представления. Для эффективного инференса используется глобальный эмбеддинг на основе токена [EOS], а средний пулинг факторизованных латентов применяется для переранжирования короткого списка кандидатов, что почти не добавляет вычислительных затрат.", "emoji": "🖼️", "title": "Сначала представь изменение, потом ищи картинку"}

Abstract ToolSpace constructs realistic multi-environment tool-use benchmarks by mining execution traces, clustering tool compositions, and validating tasks through replayable state-aware random walks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models are deployed as long-horizon agents that compose diverse tools across MCP services into executable workflows. Existing MCP environment-scaling methods are data-centric: they first synthesize a database and scenario, then derive tools and tasks from them. As a result, tool interfaces may not match real ones, tool compositions are not grounded in execution, and tasks rarely span more than one environment. We propose ToolSpace, a tool-centric framework that reverses this order. ToolSpace mines data, state, and retry dependencies from adjacent calls in execution traces of real MCP services and open agent corpora, using deterministic rules rather than LLM judgment, and clusters the resulting weighted tool-composition graph into environments. Databases are synthesized only for state-mutating environments, while query and stateless tools replay recorded returns. ToolSpace then performs state-aware random walks with rollback and decompiles each chain into a natural-language task with assertion-based checks, keeping only samples that replay successfully from the initial state. Applied to AutomationBench and tau3-Bench Banking, the pipeline produces harder Pro versions on which 7 frontier models drop by up to 38.2 points. Across seven long-horizon tool-use benchmarks, ToolSpace raises Qwen3.6-35B-A3B from 23.0 to 41.0 average score (43.2 with RL) and GLM-5.2 from 44.6 to 56.6, on par with Claude Opus 5. Ablations show that execution-witnessed edges clearly outperform LLM-judged ones, and performance keeps improving with data and environment scale.
[04.11.2026 12:49] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет ToolSpace — фреймворк, который строит реалистичные бенчмарки для LLM-агентов, использующих инструменты MCP-сервисов. В отличие от подходов, где сначала синтезируется база данных, здесь зависимости между вызовами инструментов извлекаются из реальных execution traces детерминированными правилами, а не оценками LLM. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract ToolSpace constructs realistic multi-environment tool-use benchmarks by mining execution traces, clustering tool compositions, and validating tasks through replayable state-aware random walks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language models are deployed as long-horizon agents that compose diverse tools across MCP services into executable workflows. Existing MCP environment-scaling methods are data-centric: they first synthesize a database and scenario, then derive tools and tasks from them. As a result, tool interfaces may not match real ones, tool compositions are not grounded in execution, and tasks rarely span more than one environment. We propose ToolSpace, a tool-centric framework that reverses this order. ToolSpace mines data, state, and retry dependencies from adjacent calls in execution traces of real MCP services and open agent corpora, using deterministic rules rather than LLM judgment, and clusters the resulting weighted tool-composition graph into environments. Databases are synthesized only for state-mutating environments, while query and stateless tools replay recorded returns. ToolSpace then performs state-aware random walks with rollback and decompiles each chain into a natural-language task with assertion-based checks, keeping only samples that replay successfully from the initial state. Applied to AutomationBench and tau3-Bench Banking, the pipeline produces harder Pro versions on which 7 frontier models drop by up to 38.2 points. Across seven long-horizon tool-use benchmarks, ToolSpace raises Qwen3.6-35B-A3B from 23.0 to 41.0 average score (43.2 with RL) and GLM-5.2 from 44.6 to 56.6, on par with Claude Opus 5. Ablations show that execution-witnessed edges clearly outperform LLM-judged ones, and performance keeps improving with data and environment scale."

Abstract YANAMI introduces a large-scale multimodal dialogue dataset and benchmark for stateful interaction, requiring models to track and realize evolving dialogue states across turns, and reveals that current unified multimodal models still struggle with this task.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Unified Multimodal Models (UMMs) integrate visual understanding, generation, and editing within a single unified framework. However, existing unified multimodal datasets and benchmarks remain dominated by single-turn tasks. Even in recent multi-turn datasets, many turns can be solved independently without tracking information across turns. Consequently, they provide limited supervision and evaluation for multimodal stateful interaction, which requires models to understand, maintain, update, and faithfully realize evolving dialogue states across turns. To address this gap, we introduce YANAMI, the first large-scale multimodal dialogue dataset and benchmark for stateful interaction, comprising YANAMI-400K and YANAMI-Bench. YANAMI-400K contains 400K multi-turn multimodal dialogues, with more than 2M images and 2.6M dialogue turns. The dialogues span four representative scenarios: Multi-Image Fusion, Image Editing, World Modeling, and Visual Reasoning, and require models to continuously track, preserve, and revise evolving multimodal dialogue states across turns. YANAMI-Bench comprises 400 multi-turn dialogues for systematically evaluating multimodal stateful interaction. It jointly evaluates Dialogue State Understanding and Visual Realization, characterizing the gap between models' ability to understand evolving dialogue states and their ability to faithfully realize them in visual outputs. Experiments show that fine-tuning BAGEL on YANAMI-400K improves image editing, generation, and multimodal stateful interaction by 10.3%, 2.0%, and 13.23%, respectively, while preserving its general visual understanding capabilities. Evaluations on YANAMI-Bench further reveal that existing UMMs still struggle to maintain, update, and visually realize evolving multimodal dialogue states.

Авторы также создали бенчмарк YANAMI-Bench для оценки понимания состояния диалога (Dialogue State Understanding) и визуальной реализации (Visual Realization). Abstract EvoTeam evolves multi-agent collaboration by jointly adapting team organization, interaction rules, and agent skills through execution experience while maintaining reusable team libraries.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on collaboration among specialized agents to solve complex tasks. Existing methods improve MAS by refining the prompts, skills, and execution strategies of individual agents based on task feedback. However, improving individual agents does not necessarily lead to better team collaboration. Recent studies have therefore extended evolution to the collaboration level, using collective execution experience to adjust team organization, and inter-agent interactions. Nevertheless, evolving collaboration across heterogeneous tasks remains challenging, as existing approaches often constrain team reorganization within a shared structure and reuse interaction rules across incompatible team contexts. In this paper, we propose EvoTeam, a collaboration-centric evolution framework that jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience. It maintains a library of reusable teams, selecting or creating agent teams and guiding collaboration through team-specific interaction rules. Collaboration experience accumulates within compatible team contexts, while individual skills remain reusable across teams. Experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam.
[04.11.2026 12:49] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет EvoTeam — фреймворк, который эволюционирует коллаборацию в мультиагентных LLM-системах, а не только отдельных агентов. Он совместно адаптирует структуру команды, правила взаимодействия и навыки агентов на основе опыта выполнения задач. Для этого используется библиотека переиспользуемых команд, из которой выбираются или создаются новые команды с собственными правилами взаимодействия. Эксперименты на четырёх бенчмарках показывают эффективность подхода.", "emoji": "🤝", "title": "Учим не только агентов, но и их команду работать вместе"}

"Abstract EvoTeam evolves multi-agent collaboration by jointly adapting team organization, interaction rules, and agent skills through execution experience while maintaining reusable team libraries.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on collaboration among specialized agents to solve complex tasks. Existing methods improve MAS by refining the prompts, skills, and execution strategies of individual agents based on task feedback. However, improving individual agents does not necessarily lead to better team collaboration. Recent studies have therefore extended evolution to the collaboration level, using collective execution experience to adjust team organization, and inter-agent interactions. Nevertheless, evolving collaboration across heterogeneous tasks remains challenging, as existing approaches often constrain team reorganization within a shared structure and reuse interaction rules across incompatible team contexts. In this paper, we propose EvoTeam, a collaboration-centric evolution framework that jointly evolves team organization, interaction rules, and individual agent skills through execution experience. It maintains a library of reusable teams, selecting or creating agent teams and guiding collaboration through team-specific interaction rules. Collaboration experience accumulates within compatible team contexts, while individual skills remain reusable across teams. Experiments across four benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness. Our code is available at https://github.com/DEEP-JLU/EvoTeam."

Abstract VidUEU-Agent automates instruction data synthesis from raw videos via a multi-stage pipeline with autonomous iteration, yielding a 100K-sample dataset that improves multimodal model performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small The advancement of multimodal learning is heavily constrained by the scarcity and high annotation cost of high-quality instruction data. While raw videos offer an abundant and dynamic source of visual knowledge, transforming them into precise instruction data remains highly challenging. To bridge this gap, we present VidUEU-Agent, an automated pipeline that synthesizes instruction data from raw videos for multimodal understanding, editing, and unified tasks. VidUEU-Agent follows a four-stage pipeline: it performs global perception to filter low-quality shots, mines keyframes and multimodal signals, constructs metadata and routes the metadata to synthesize instruction samples. More importantly, our agent framework can autonomously iterate the data distribution based on downstream training results, thereby optimizing data quality, balance, and training effectiveness. Scaling this pipeline, we construct VidUEU-100K, a large-scale dataset of 100K training samples spanning multiple domains and tasks, providing a robust foundation for developing comprehensive multimodal capabilities. Extensive experiments demonstrate that fine-tuning baseline models on VidUEU-100K yields significant and consistent performance gains across diverse representative benchmarks, validating the effectiveness of our proposed agent framework and dataset.

Пайплайн состоит из четырёх этапов: глобальное восприятие для фильтрации низкокачественных шотов, извлечение ключевых кадров и мультимодальных сигналов, построение метаданных и синтез инструкционных примеров. Abstract WebHarbor provides 153 executable, dockerized web environments for stable evaluation and reinforcement learning training of GUI agents, enabling strong generalization across multi-step and post-authentication tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small GUI web agents require realistic and interactive environments that remain stable during both evaluation and training. Live website evaluation suffers from changing content, access restrictions, and limited coverage of authenticated workflows, while existing synthetic environments for training often lack functional depth and visual fidelity. We propose WebHarbor, a suite of 153 executable and dockerized website environments that faithfully reconstruct the visual interfaces, content, and functional workflows of real-world web services under deterministic control. We build WebHarbor through task-driven human-agent collaboration, where environments evolve as new tasks expand their functional scope, coding agents implement the required changes, and human contributors audit visual fidelity, functional correctness, and task validity. The resulting suite comprises 3,518 tasks spanning multi-step and post-authentication workflows, where existing works often fail to cover. Beyond reliable evaluation, WebHarbor provides high-fidelity synthetic web environments for cost-efficient reinforcement learning (RL). We demonstrate that a Qwen 4B-level GUI agent trained with RL in WebHarbor generalizes to unseen tasks and performs competitively with leading open-source GUI web agents across four benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/aiming-lab/WebHarbor.

Окружения создавались совместно людьми и coding agents, а итогом стали 3 518 задач, включая многошаговые сценарии и сценарии после авторизации. Такие среды позволяют стабильно оценивать GUI agents и обучать их с помощью reinforcement learning без рисков, связанных с изменяющимся контентом живых сайтов. Эксперименты показывают, что агент уровня Qwen 4B, обученный в WebHarbor, хорошо обобщается на новые задачи и конкурирует с ведущими open-source агентами на четырёх бенчмарках.", "emoji": "🌐", "title": "Стабильные веб-полигоны для обучения и оценки GUI-агентов"}

Abstract Caper pre-executes shell commands in isolated sandboxes to evaluate real-world effects before approval, reducing harmful outcomes compared to text-based approvers.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Coding agents increasingly rely on automated approvers to decide whether a proposed shell command may run. Existing approvers, from rule-based checkers to guardrail models and deployed permission classifiers, base their decisions on the text of the request and command. Yet a command's behavior depends on the code it invokes and the state of the workspace, and systems that observe file changes leave the assessment to the agent or a human after execution. We present Caper, a consequence-aware approval mechanism that pre-executes each command in a snapshotted sandbox, provides an independent approver with a bounded report of its observed effects, commits allowed commands, and rolls back blocked ones with a reason the agent can act on. Compared with six existing systems, Caper achieves the lowest harmful-outcome rate on SABER (6.7% versus at least 14.8%) and YoloFS-Ext, a benchmark we build in which harmful behavior is hidden inside invoked tools (7.8% versus at least 35.3%), while blocking 1.0% of the safe twins of the dangerous tasks in YoloFS-Ext.

Независимый approver (классификатор или LLM) получает ограниченный отчёт о наблюдаемых эффектах, а не только текст запроса, что важно, поскольку поведение команды зависит от вызываемого кода и состояния рабочей среды. Abstract MoLRSI is an AI agent that optimizes molecular design workflows and retains cross-task memory to improve search policies, achieving higher experimental hit rates in antibiotic and peptide binder discovery than expert-led methods.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Molecular discovery requires navigating vast combinatorial search spaces while balancing competing biological objectives. AI agents can automate expert-defined molecular design protocols, but automation alone does not address how to optimize workflows for new tasks or improve their development through experience. Here we introduce MoLRSI, an AI agent that combines systematic optimization of workflows and model architectures with persistent cross-task memory to recursively improve its search policy for molecular discovery. Workflow performance, refinement outcomes, and execution feedback guide subsequent searches, allowing the agent to improve both its workflows and its strategy for developing them. We evaluate MoLRSI in two challenging molecular discovery settings that require balancing competing properties. In antibiotic discovery, MoLRSI developed a screening workflow balancing antibacterial activity, cytotoxicity and structural novelty, achieving an experimental hit rate of 15.9%, compared with 8.7% for a previous expert-led workflow using the same training data and screening library. In de novo peptide-MHC binder design, MoLRSI developed workflows that jointly consider target binding and specificity, producing eight experimentally validated hits against MART-1 and SARS-CoV-1 pMHCs with preferential binding over tested off-targets while Claude Science completely failed. These findings demonstrate how experience-guided workflow optimization can address competing discovery objectives while improving the search strategies used in subsequent campaigns.

Abstract FlowTranslator is a training-free framework for in-image machine translation that uses diffusion trajectory controls to generate accurate translated text within images without paired training data.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In-image machine translation (IIMT) replaces text in an image with its translation while preserving the surrounding visual context. Existing task-specific models achieve convincing performance but require massive paired training images before and after translation. As advanced text-to-image (T2I) models have reached a practically usable level of multilingual text generation, we argue that this capability can be transferred to IIMT and present FlowTranslator. It is a training-free IIMT framework that first employs an OCR model and an LLM to generate the translation prompt, which is injected into the T2I-based diffusion model. To optimize the accurate and natural generation of translated text, we introduce two trajectory controls: Region Coupling Release (RCR) partially decorrelates target-branch noise inside the text region early in the trajectory, while Coupling Expansion (CE) amplifies the emerging edit displacement mid-trajectory. Under the EasyOCR read-back protocol, FlowTranslator achieves average PRIM BLEU and COMET scores of 20.6 and 57.2, respectively, substantially outperforming the strong training-based baseline UniTranslator. On the more challenging and realistic whole-image ScenePair photographs translated into Chinese, it reaches 40.1\% exact-match accuracy and surpasses existing editing systems, some of which only address the text-rendering stage rather than the complete IIMT task. These results establish flow-based editing as a strong, fully training-free baseline for IIMT.

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract FlowTranslator is a training-free framework for in-image machine translation that uses diffusion trajectory controls to generate accurate translated text within images without paired training data.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In-image machine translation (IIMT) replaces text in an image with its translation while preserving the surrounding visual context. Existing task-specific models achieve convincing performance but require massive paired training images before and after translation. As advanced text-to-image (T2I) models have reached a practically usable level of multilingual text generation, we argue that this capability can be transferred to IIMT and present FlowTranslator. It is a training-free IIMT framework that first employs an OCR model and an LLM to generate the translation prompt, which is injected into the T2I-based diffusion model. To optimize the accurate and natural generation of translated text, we introduce two trajectory controls: Region Coupling Release (RCR) partially decorrelates target-branch noise inside the text region early in the trajectory, while Coupling Expansion (CE) amplifies the emerging edit displacement mid-trajectory. Under the EasyOCR read-back protocol, FlowTranslator achieves average PRIM BLEU and COMET scores of 20.6 and 57.2, respectively, substantially outperforming the strong training-based baseline UniTranslator. On the more challenging and realistic whole-image ScenePair photographs translated into Chinese, it reaches 40.1\% exact-match accuracy and surpasses existing editing systems, some of which only address the text-rendering stage rather than the complete IIMT task. These results establish flow-based editing as a strong, fully training-free baseline for IIMT."

[04.11.2026 12:50] OpenRouter request. Model: anthropic/claude-haiku-5.5. Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract A benchmark for resuming interrupted software tasks reveals performance drops from interleaved work, and a context-management module improves recovery.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Software development sessions often involve several unfinished tasks rather than one issue carried through to completion. As coding agents take on repository-level development, they need to recover an earlier task's requirements and partial progress after intervening work. Existing repository-level benchmarks typically score one issue-to-patch episode and do not directly measure whether an agent can resume a task after advancing other unfinished work in the same repository. We introduce \ourmethod, a benchmark for focus-task resumption in sessions that interleave unfinished tasks drawn from the same source repository, with a persistent checkout for each task. Each focus task is evaluated in matched uninterrupted and switched sessions with identical focus requirements and executable verifiers. The switched session interleaves ordered stages from other unfinished tasks in the same repository, while only the final focus patch is scored. \ourmethod~contains 200 focus tasks from 19 Python repositories, reconstructed from issue, pull-request, and review histories and rendered as 400 matched session specifications. Across 7 coding agents, interleaved unfinished work produces an average focus-task resolution gap of 11.5 percentage points, ranging from 6.0 to 15.0 percentage points. Trajectory analysis identifies cross-task requirement confusion and loss of task state as recurrent resumption behaviors. We introduce a plug-in context-management module that selects task-relevant history and maintains concise task-state summaries. On OpenCode, the module improves focus-task completion under same-repository task switching by 5.5 to 15.5 percentage points across the evaluated models. Code is available at github.com/Miraclecsc/Switch-SWE.
[04.11.2026 12:50] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют новый бенчмарк для проверки того, способны ли LLM-агенты для разработки ПО возобновлять прерванную задачу после работы над другими незавершёнными задачами в том же репозитории. Оценка проводится на 200 задачах из 19 Python-репозиториев, построенных на основе issue и pull-request, в парных сессиях без переключений и с переключениями. Во всех 7 протестированных агентах переключение задач снижает долю решённых задач в среднем на 11,5 п.п., что выявляет смешение требований между задачами и потерю состояния задачи. Предложенный модуль управления контекстом, который выбирает релевантную историю и поддерживает краткие саммари состояния задач, повышает качество на 5,5–15,5 п.п.", "emoji": "🔀", "title": "Не потерять нить: возвращение к задаче после переключения контекста"}
